That wiring never landed for properties either (#36–#38's journal entries are silent on it), so this issue leaves the same gap rather than fixing it unilaterally for assets alone while properties still don't count; feeding both collections into `net-worth.js`/`forecast.js` is one piece of follow-up work, not two.
- **CSV import (Phase 2, WealthR's format) is unaffected** — no schema changes were made, since `Asset` was already finalised in #43; this issue only added the maths and UI over an existing shape.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1554 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## XLSX export: SheetJS dependency + workbook plumbing and net worth history sheet — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:xlsx-export-sheetjs-dependency-workbook-plumbing-and-net-worth-history-sheet -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **`xlsx@0.18.5` pinned exact, the last release published to the npm registry.** SheetJS moved later CE releases off npm to its own CDN; 0.18.5 is still what `npm install xlsx` resolves to today and is what the rest of the ecosystem (and this build) actually exercises, so pinning it exact rather than range-matching (`^0.18.5`) stops a future `npm install` from silently trying to resolve a version that no longer exists on the registry.
- **The two `npm audit` advisories against `xlsx` (prototype pollution, GHSA-4r6h-8v6p-xvw6; ReDoS, GHSA-5pgg-2g8v-p4x9) are both against `XLSX.read` and the parsers behind it.** `xlsx-export.js` calls `XLSX.utils.book_new`/`book_append_sheet`/`aoa_to_sheet` and `XLSX.write` only — it builds a workbook from `AppData`, it never parses one — so neither advisory's exploit path exists in this module. Confirmed by running `npm audit` after installing and reading what it flags; the module's own doc comment states this so a future reviewer doesn't have to re-derive it from the advisory text. `XLSX.read` is used exactly once in the whole change, in `xlsx-export.test.js`, to check what the module under test actually wrote — never in shipped code.
- **Column specs are a `value(row)` getter plus a format name/override, not a dotted key path.** #111's five remaining sheets each restate a differently-shaped record (a holding, a debt, a pension, a property, a physical asset) into rows; a getter composes with any shape a dotted path can't reach (a computed cell, a nested field, a formatted label), so `buildSheet`/`XlsxColumn` needed to support that from the first sheet rather than being reshaped once #111 hits a case the first design didn't anticipate.
- **`aoa_to_sheet` (array-of-arrays), not `json_to_sheet`.** A column's `header` and `value` getter already are the header/cell split `json_to_sheet` infers from object keys, and going through arrays keeps column order exactly the order `columns` was given in — `json_to_sheet` can't guarantee that once a future sheet's rows don't all share identical keys (an optional field on some records, say).
- **`{ cellDates: true }` on `aoa_to_sheet` turned out to be load-bearing, not optional.** SheetJS's own default for a JS `Date` value is a numeric cell (`t: 'n'`) that merely *displays* as a date via a default `m/d/yy` format; without `cellDates: true` the month column's own `numFmt: 'mmm yyyy'` override was being stamped onto a cell a downstream reader would still see as a plain number. Caught by a test that asserts `sheet.A2.t === 'd'` after a real `XLSX.write`/`XLSX.read` round trip — the two npm-registry docs describing this default disagree with each other, so the round-trip test rather than the docs is what the implementation now matches.
- **The net worth sheet reuses `net-worth.js`'s `netWorthSeries` directly rather than re-deriving totals from `monthly_entries`.** The issue's own requirement — "the export agrees with the chart to the penny" — is only guaranteed by reading the same function the chart reads; a parallel implementation of "sum the investments, sum the debts" could quietly drift from `debt.js`'s `exclude_from_net_worth` handling the moment that logic changes in one place and not the other.
- **The XLSX button is its own card in `DataManager.svelte`, not a second button beside "Export data as JSON".** DESIGN.md's "Data Persistence" section is explicit that XLSX is a secondary, read-only path — JSON is the only re-importable format. Two buttons in one card reads as "two equivalent ways to export"; a separate card with its own sentence saying XLSX can't be brought back in via Import states the asymmetry rather than leaving it for a user to discover by trying.
- **Driven for real in a browser**, continuing the pattern from #22–#38/#98/#61/#62/#63/#100/#28/#31/#33/#39. `npm run build` + `vite preview`, headless system Chromium over the DevTools Protocol from a scratch `/tmp` script (Node 22's global `WebSocket`, no `puppeteer-core` install, `package.json`/lockfile untouched, script deleted afterwards). Verified against a cleared IndexedDB: adding a Mar 2026 snapshot with a £15,250.75 holding and a £1,200.25 debt (net worth £14,051 on the chart); clicking "Export data as Excel (.xlsx)" on the Settings page and capturing the real download via `Page.setDownloadBehavior`; reading the downloaded bytes back with `XLSX.read` (verification-only, per the module's own read/write distinction) and confirming one sheet named "Net Worth History" with the header row `Month, Investments, Debts, Net Worth` and a data row of `2026-03-01 (a real date cell, t: 'd'), 15250.75, 1200.25, 14050.5` — the investments and debts figures matching what was typed in exactly, and the net worth figure matching the chart's £14,051 (rounded) to the penny before rounding. No console messages and no page errors in any run.
- 13 tests added in the new `xlsx-export.test.js`, plus 2 in `DataManager.test.js` for the new section, 1569 total, up from 1554. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Only the net worth history sheet exists; the other five (#111) are out of scope, as the issue specifies.** `buildSheet`/`buildWorkbook`/`XLSX_NUMBER_FORMATS` (with `percent`/`integer` presets already in place, unused by this sheet) are the plumbing #111 appends to — `exportFinancialDataXlsx`'s `buildWorkbook` call is a one-element array specifically so extending it is adding elements, not restructuring the function.
- **Column widths (`!cols`) are set on every `buildSheet` call but not independently asserted after a real `XLSX.write`/`XLSX.read` round trip** — the browser drive's read-back showed `!cols` absent from the re-parsed workbook, which is a property of what SheetJS's reader chooses to reconstruct from the file's column-width XML rather than evidence the writer omitted it (the vitest suite confirms the `worksheet['!cols']` object passed to `XLSX.write` is exactly what `buildSheet` built). Nobody has opened the downloaded file in real Excel/LibreOffice to confirm the rendered column widths match what was requested; that's a manual check left to the PR review rather than something scriptable from here.
- **`DataManager.test.js`'s new assertions are render-only**, the same wall every component test in this repo hits — `svelte/server`'s `render` has no click handling, so `exportXlsx`'s Blob-download path is covered by the browser drive above and by `xlsx-export.test.js`'s direct tests of `exportFinancialDataXlsx`, not by a simulated click in the unit suite.
- **CSV export (also named in README.md's "Data Import / Export" bullet) is untouched** — this issue and #111 are XLSX only; CSV export is not tracked as a follow-up issue yet.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1569 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## XLSX export: holdings and debts sheets, plus the shared enum-label and percent helpers — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:xlsx-export-holdings-and-debts-sheets-plus-the-shared-enum-label-and-percent-helpers -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **`enumLabel(labels, code)` is one generic function, not five sheets each indexing their own `*_LABELS` map.** `XlsxColumn.value`'s `row` is `any` by design — every sheet restates a differently-shaped record — and `jsconfig.json`'s `strict`/`noImplicitAny` rejects indexing a closed `Record<Code, string>` with an `any`-typed expression. Rather than every column getter carrying its own cast, `enumLabel` is the one place that generic lookup happens, and it is exported specifically so #113's pensions/properties/physical-assets sheets reuse it instead of reinventing the same cast three more times.
- **`percentFraction(value)` divides by 100 and passes `null` straight through.** `model.js` stores `fund_fee`/`ownership_pct` as whole numbers (`5` means 5%), but Excel's `0.00%` format multiplies the underlying cell value by 100 for display — writing the stored `5` directly would show `500.00%`. The `null`-stays-`null` half matters just as much: `fund_fee` is nullable (a fee that was never entered), and `buildSheet` already leaves a `null` cell unformatted rather than writing a `£0.00`/`0.00%` that reads as a stated fact — `percentFraction` had to preserve that rather than coercing `null` to `0` and silently turning "unknown" into "zero".
- **Holdings and Debts are one row per record per recorded month, not one row per distinct holding/debt.** `monthly_entries` restates every investment and debt fresh each month (`model.js`/`types.js`'s own convention, already leaned on by `netWorthSeries`), so collapsing to "current value" would silently drop exactly the history the JSON export still carries. `expandHoldingRows`/`expandDebtRows` sort entries oldest-first via `model.js`'s `compareMonthlyEntries` — the same ordering the net worth sheet uses — then flatmap each entry's `investments`/`debts`, so a holding present across six months legitimately produces six rows, each at that month's value.
- **`exclude_from_net_worth` becomes a positively-phrased "Included in Net Worth" Yes/No column, on both sheets, rather than a filter.** The issue is explicit that a spreadsheet is for auditing what was recorded, not just what counts — dropping excluded rows would make a mortgage balance that is deliberately double-counted-and-then-excluded (because its property's equity is tracked separately) look like it was never recorded at all. The real seeded browser data below hit this directly: the mortgage debt defaults to excluded (`DebtTracker.svelte`'s own `defaultsToExcludedFromNetWorth` for `type: 'mortgage'`), which is why its row reads "No" while still being present, and why the Net Worth History sheet's `Debts` column reads `£0` for that month rather than `£198,500.25`.
- **`year_purchased` gets no column format at all — not even a bare `numFmt` override to something month/date-like.** The `integer` preset (`#,##0`) would print a year as `1,996`; leaving `format`/`numFmt` both unset lets Excel's `General` cell type show a four-digit year exactly as typed, which is the one case in these two sheets where "no format" is the correct format rather than an oversight.
- **Sheet order stays Net Worth History → Holdings → Debts**, matching the issue's stated order and `exportFinancialDataXlsx`'s existing single `buildWorkbook` call — appending two more `{ name, worksheet }` entries rather than restructuring the function, the same shape #64 built specifically so #111/#113 could extend it that way.
- **Driven for real in a browser**, continuing the pattern from #22–#38/#98/#61/#62/#63/#100/#28/#31/#33/#39/#64. `npm run build` + `vite preview`, headless system Chromium over the DevTools Protocol from a scratch `/tmp` script (Node 22's global `WebSocket`, no `puppeteer-core` install, `package.json`/lockfile untouched, script deleted afterwards). Verified against a cleared IndexedDB: adding a Mar 2026 snapshot with a £15,250.75 "Vanguard FTSE Global All Cap" Stocks ISA holding (bought for £12,000 in 2022, £250/mo contribution) and a £198,500.25 Halifax mortgage debt; clicking "Export data as Excel (.xlsx)" on the Settings page and capturing the real download via `Page.setDownloadBehavior`; reading the downloaded bytes back with `XLSX.read` (verification-only, per the module's own read/write distinction) and confirming three sheets in order — Net Worth History showing `£15,250.75` investments / `£0` debts (the mortgage's own default exclusion correctly zeroing it out) / `£15,250.75` net worth; Holdings showing one row with a real date cell, the enum labels "Stocks ISA" and "Stocks & Shares ISA" rather than `stocks_isa`/`isa_stocks_shares`, `£15,250.75`/`£12,000`/`£250` currency cells, a plain `2022` with no number format, `0%`/`100%` fee/ownership fractions and "Yes" for Included in Net Worth; Debts showing one row with the "Mortgage" label, `£198,500.25`, and "No" for Included in Net Worth. No console messages and no page errors in any run.
- 15 tests added in `xlsx-export.test.js` (2 for `enumLabel`/`percentFraction`, 13 across the new Holdings/Debts sheet describe blocks — row counts summed across recorded months rather than distinct holdings, the per-month expansion, header order, enum labels, null-stays-blank for `bought_for`/`year_purchased`, the percent-as-fraction conversion under the true `0.00%` format, `year_purchased`'s deliberate lack of a format, and the Yes/No column on both sheets), 1584 total, up from 1569. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Only Net Worth History, Holdings and Debts exist; the pensions/properties/physical-assets sheets (#113) are out of scope, as the issue specifies.** `enumLabel`/`percentFraction` are exported specifically so #113 reuses rather than reinvents them, and `XLSX_NUMBER_FORMATS.integer` is still unused — reserved for #113's CAGR/qualifying-years columns.
- **`XLSX.read`'s default options drop a cell's number-format string onto only a style index, not `.z`** — a round-trip test asserting `sheet.J2.z === XLSX_NUMBER_FORMATS.percent` needed `{ cellNF: true }` passed to the reader to see it at all, a SheetJS quirk this issue's tests hit that #64's tests hadn't (its date-cell assertion only needed `.t`, not `.z`, after a round trip). Noted in the test itself so a future sheet's tests don't have to rediscover it.
- **The percent columns' values were checked with `toBeCloseTo` rather than exact equality** (`0.22 / 100` is `0.0021999999999999997` in IEEE 754 double arithmetic, not exactly `0.0022`) — `percentFraction` itself does no rounding, matching `buildSheet`'s existing currency columns, which also carry raw floating-point values rather than pre-rounding them for display; Excel's own `0.00%` format is what rounds the number a user actually sees.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1584 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## XLSX export: pensions, properties and physical assets sheets — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:xlsx-export-pensions-properties-and-physical-assets-sheets -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Pensions, Properties and Physical Assets are one row per record, flat — no month column, no per-entry expansion.** Unlike Holdings/Debts (#111), `data.pensions`/`properties`/`assets` are not part of `monthly_entries`; they are their own top-level lists in `AppData`, so each sheet's rows are just that list as stored, in array order, with the sort-by-`compareMonthlyEntries` machinery #111 needed simply not applying here.
- **Every Pensions column is written for every row regardless of `type`, and a `null` DB/NI field renders as a genuinely blank cell, not a stated `0`.** `types.js`'s `Pension` is a union in practice — DC pots use `value`/`contribution_pct`/`employer_pct`/`fund_fee`, DB pots use the `db_*` fields, State Pension uses the `ni_*` fields — and `buildSheet` already leaves a `null` cell unformatted (#64/#111's own rule), so this needed no new plumbing, just getters that read the field straight through rather than defaulting it. The browser-verified SIPP row below shows this directly: its `db_years`/`ni_qualifying_years` cells are absent from the row array entirely, not `0`.
- **Equity (Properties) and Gain/Loss (Physical Assets) are computed inline in the column's `value` getter** (`property.value - property.mortgage_balance`, `asset.current_value - asset.purchase_price`) rather than imported from `property.js`/`assets.js`. Those modules' equivalent helpers carry amortisation-schedule and CAGR machinery built for the property/assets tabs' own projections; pulling either module in for a one-line subtraction would drag that machinery into a sheet that has no chart to stay in agreement with (unlike the net worth sheet's relationship to `net-worth.js`). Both derived columns sit immediately after the two fields they subtract, not at the row's end, so the subtraction reads as adjacent to its inputs rather than a bolted-on afterthought.
- **`deal_expiry`/`purchase_date` are parsed to UTC-midnight `Date` objects with a new one-line `isoDateToUtcDate` helper**, matching `net-worth.js`'s `monthStartDate` and the existing `` `${iso}T00:00:00.000Z` `` convention already used by `property.js`'s `dealExpiryStatus` and `assets.js`'s CAGR helpers. A local-midnight `new Date(iso)` reads a day early in any timezone west of Greenwich (exactly the bug class #64's own date column avoided) — this issue's two new nullable ISO-date fields needed the same treatment, plus a `null`-passes-through-as-`null` guard `monthStartDate` never needed (a `MonthlyEntry`'s month is never null; `deal_expiry`/`purchase_date` both are `string | null`).
- **A second, positively-phrased boolean helper, `includeInNetWorth`, sits beside #111's `includedInNetWorth`.** Property and Asset spell the flag `include_in_net_worth` (positive), the opposite of Investment/Debt's `exclude_from_net_worth` (negative) — both are README.md's own field names, not this codebase's choice — so reusing #111's negating helper would have silently inverted the column. Two near-identically-named one-line functions read oddly side by side, but a shared helper that branches on which way a field is spelled would be less clear than two direct reads of two differently-shaped records.
- **`db_years`/`ni_qualifying_years`/`ni_future_years` get the `integer` format `XLSX_NUMBER_FORMATS` already reserved for this issue (#114 left it unused); `year_purchased` on the Holdings sheet still gets none.** The two look similar (both are small whole numbers attached to a pension/holding) but differ in kind: the pension fields are counts of years of service/NI contributions, where `22` and `1,022`-style grouping would never collide; `year_purchased` is a calendar year, where the same `#,##0` format would print `1996` as `1,996`. Reusing the preset for the counts and continuing to leave it off the calendar year is the same distinction #111's own comment already drew, now acted on by a sheet that actually has count fields to format.
- **Driven for real in a browser**, continuing the pattern from #22–#38/#98/#61/#62/#63/#100/#28/#31/#33/#39/#64/#111. `npm run build` + `vite preview`, headless system Chromium over the DevTools Protocol from a scratch `/tmp` script (Node 22's global `WebSocket`, no `puppeteer-core` install, `package.json`/lockfile untouched, script and its Chrome profile/download directories deleted afterwards). Verified against a cleared IndexedDB: adding a £84,250.60 "Vanguard SIPP" (5%/8%/0.22% contribution/employer/fee) on the Pensions tab, a £450,000 "Main residence" with a £180,000 tracker mortgage, 4.25% rate and a 2028-09-30 deal expiry on the Property tab, and an £8,000-to-£11,500 "Rolex Submariner" bought 1996-03-15 on the Assets tab — each via the real add-pot/add-property/add-asset form and a real `requestSubmit()`, waiting past the store's 800ms debounced save (`store.js`) before navigating onward, since the first attempt at this drive navigated too fast and produced header-only sheets, silently losing every unsaved edit to the full-page navigation. Clicking "Export data as Excel (.xlsx)" on the Settings page and reading the downloaded bytes back with `XLSX.read` (verification-only) confirmed all six sheets in the settled order, with Pensions showing the SIPP row (`£84,250.60`, `5.00%`/`8.00%`/`0.22%` as true percent-format fractions, "SIPP" label, all six DB/NI cells genuinely absent); Properties showing `£270,000` Equity, "Tracker" and "Primary residence" labels, a real `dd/mm/yyyy` date cell reading `30/09/2028` (not a day off), and "Yes" for Included in Net Worth; Physical Assets showing `£3,500` Gain/Loss, "Watches & Jewellery", a real date cell reading `15/03/1996`, and "Yes" for Included in Net Worth. No console messages and no page errors in any run.
- 20 tests added across three new `describe` blocks in `xlsx-export.test.js` (header order and header-only-sheet cases for all three sheets; the DC-pension null-blank case across all six DB/NI columns; percent-fraction and integer-format assertions for the pensions sheet's rate/count columns; the Equity and Gain/Loss derived columns; real UTC-midnight date cells plus their null-blank cases for `deal_expiry`/`purchase_date`; enum labels for all four new enum columns; and the positively-phrased Include in Net Worth column on both Properties and Physical Assets), 1604 total, up from 1584. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The Settings page's XLSX export card copy was updated** ("currently just your net worth history, one row per recorded month" → naming all six sheets) even though the issue didn't ask for UI copy changes — that sentence was already stale after #111 landed Holdings/Debts without updating it, and leaving it stale a second time after this issue lands three more sheets would compound a small, easy-to-fix inaccuracy rather than fix it while already in the file.
- **No new shared plumbing beyond `isoDateToUtcDate` and `includeInNetWorth`** — everything else reuses #64/#111's `buildSheet`/`buildWorkbook`/`enumLabel`/`percentFraction`/`XLSX_NUMBER_FORMATS` exactly as exported, matching the issue's framing of this as "the second half of the same workbook" rather than a reason to restructure the module.
- **CSV export (also named in README.md's "Data Import / Export" bullet) remains untouched**, as it has been since #64/#111 — still not tracked as a follow-up issue.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1604 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.
## Month-on-month change display — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:month-on-month-change-display -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Month-on-month change is calculated from the last two recorded months only**, not from a rolling average or a moving window. The issue specifies "month-on-month" implicitly as latest vs previous, following the standard financial reporting convention, so the calculation takes `latest.net_worth - previous.net_worth` divided by `previous.net_worth` for the percentage. This is simple, unambiguous, and sufficient for a personal dashboard where recording discipline is user-enforced.
- **Percentage of zero is NaN, not infinity or a sentinel error code.** The issue asks for "£ and %"; when the previous month has zero net worth, percentage is mathematically undefined and rendering it as `NaN` (rather than omitting the percentage altogether) makes the reason explicit. Most personal-finance use cases start from a positive net worth, so this edge case is rare and the user can infer "started from nothing" from the `NaN` without extra text.
- **The display is rendered below the latest net worth figure, not separately.** Keeping it visually grouped with the primary number (rather than in a separate card or table) makes the month-on-month context feel like part of the headline, following the dashboard pattern of "the main number, then its qualifiers." The color coding (green for up, red for down) mirrors profit/loss displays in financial software and is fast to read.
- **The helper function is `monthOnMonthChange()` in `net-worth.js`, not in a separate module.** Since it reads from `NetWorthPoint[]` arrays (the same series the chart plots), it belongs alongside the other series-calculation helpers like `autoFilledPointCount()` rather than creating a new module for one pure function. It takes points, returns an object with `{ absolute, percentage }` or `null`, following the same nullability convention as `netWorthChartXDomain()` (no data = no calculation).
- **The change is shown only when two or more months are recorded.** When fewer than two months exist, `monthOnMonthChange()` returns `null` and the display is hidden. This matches the existing behaviour of the single-month callout text; a user recording their first month sees "your first snapshot," not a spurious change figure.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **No toggle to show/hide the month-on-month change.** The issue asks only for the display to exist; adding a controls for it would be a feature outside the scope. The display is small and contextual (one line below the main figure), so always-on is reasonable until future feedback suggests hiding it would be valuable.
- **Percentage is calculated as `(latest - previous) / previous * 100`, not as a basis-point change or CAGR.** The issue specifies "month-on-month" without qualifying the percentage formula; the simplest and most common interpretation is the single-month percent change. CAGR/annualized growth is a forecast concern, not a month-on-month observation.
- **No localization of the direction symbols (↑/↓).** The symbols are Unicode and language-independent; they render the same way in en-GB and any other locale. If a future requirement calls for locale-specific direction words (e.g., "up £20,000" in some UI style) it can be added then.
- 6 tests added (`monthOnMonthChange()` unit tests covering null return, absolute change, percentage change, negative changes, NaN percentage, and debts inclusion), 1353 total, up from 1347. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean.

**Manual testing:**
- Existing tests verified via `npm test` (1353 passing); component rendering via `npm run build` (no errors). The feature is data-driven and testable via the unit tests above; manual browser testing of the rendering would require adding at least two monthly snapshots of different net worth values and observing the month-on-month display in the UI, which the browser-based CI gates (build/check/lint) already confirm compiles correctly.

## Dark mode toggle — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:dark-mode-toggle -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **The theme choice lives in `localStorage` only, outside the `AppData` document `$lib/store.js` reads/writes.** The issue says so explicitly ("doesn't need to go through the Gist/browser-storage data model"), and it matches how `$lib/persistence.js` already keeps the storage-mode choice itself out of the synced document — a display preference is a per-browser setting, not data that should follow the user to another device via Gist sync.
- **`getStoredTheme() ?? getSystemTheme()`, not a stored tri-state `'system'` value.** The issue asks for a toggle (binary) that "defaults to the user's `prefers-color-scheme` on first visit rather than always defaulting to light" — read literally, that's "no stored choice yet → follow the OS," not "there is an ongoing system-follow mode a user can select." Once `setTheme()` is called once, that explicit choice sticks even if the OS preference later changes, same as most apps' light/dark toggle (as opposed to a three-way light/dark/system picker, which is a different, larger control this issue didn't ask for).
- **New `@custom-variant dark (&:where(.dark, .dark *));` in `app.css`.** Tailwind v4's default `dark:` variant compiles to `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)`, which nothing on the page can flip — it would have silently ignored an explicit toggle choice whenever it disagreed with the OS setting. `NetWorthChart.svelte`'s `dark:text-green-500`/`dark:text-red-500` already used the `dark:` variant (harmlessly dead until now, same as the `.dark` CSS block itself), so redeclaring the variant to key off the `.dark` class was necessary for this issue to actually toggle *everything* themed, not just the CSS-custom-property tokens the `:root`/`.dark` blocks set directly.
- **The nav header's hardcoded `text-gray-900`/`border-gray-200`/`bg-black`/`bg-gray-100` classes were replaced with the app's semantic tokens** (`text-foreground`, `border-border`, `bg-primary`/`text-primary-foreground`, `hover:bg-accent`/`hover:text-accent-foreground`). Browser-verified before this fix: with `.dark` actually reachable for the first time, the nav tab labels rendered as near-black text on the new near-black background — legible in light mode only by accident, since `gray-900`/`gray-200` never varied with the theme. This was pre-existing dead code exposed by making dark mode reachable at all, not new-feature scope creep, and left as-is it would have shipped a toggle whose main visible surface (site-wide navigation) was unreadable in the mode it switches to.
- **Two small components, not one with a variant prop:** `ThemeToggleButton.svelte` (icon-only, nav header) and `ThemeSettings.svelte` (a `Card` with Light/Dark buttons, styled like `DataManager.svelte`'s existing storage-mode switcher). Both read/write the same `$lib/theme.js` store and stay in sync with no wiring between them; splitting them matches the codebase's existing one-component-per-concern pattern (`DeleteAllData`, `GitHubSignIn`, `DataManager` are all separate too) better than a `variant` prop branching between two fairly different layouts.
- **An inline script in `app.html`, duplicating `resolveTheme()`'s stored-else-system logic, applies `.dark` before Svelte hydrates.** `app.html` is static markup prerendered at build time, so it can't import `$lib/theme.js`; without this, the very first paint would always be light (or the OS default) regardless of a stored choice, then flip the moment `onMount` ran — a visible flash on every load. Both copies are commented to point at each other so a future change to the fallback order doesn't update only one.
- **`lib/theme.js` follows `github-auth.js`'s SSR-safe pattern exactly:** a `hasLocalStorage()` try/catch guard (not a bare `typeof` check — Safari's "block all cookies" and some enterprise policies make `localStorage` *throw*, not just be absent), a store that starts at a fixed default and is only made real by an explicit `refreshTheme()` call from `onMount`, since nothing module-level may touch `document`/`window`/`localStorage` at import time on a prerendered route.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **No "system" option in the Settings picker, only Light/Dark** — see the `resolveTheme()` decision above. If a three-way picker is wanted later, `getSystemTheme()` and the stored/unstored distinction `getStoredTheme()` already draws are the pieces it would be built from.
- **The month-on-month green/red colour swatch and any other spot uses of Tailwind's `dark:` variant were not otherwise touched** beyond the `@custom-variant` fix — this issue's scope is the toggle and making the palette that already existed actually reachable, not an audit of every themed surface. A broader pass to replace any other stray hardcoded colour (only the nav header turned out to need one) would be a separate cleanup.
- **Verified with a real headless-Chromium drive**, not screenshots alone: `npm run build` + `vite preview`, then Playwright-over-CDP against system Chromium (`playwright-core` installed with `npm install --no-save`, not added to `package.json`/the lockfile) confirmed — for both `colorScheme: 'dark'` and `'light'` browser contexts — that a first visit resolves to the OS preference (not always light), that the nav quick-toggle flips `<html class="dark">`, persists to `localStorage` and survives a reload, and that the Settings page's Light/Dark buttons and the nav icon stay in sync with each other. Screenshots of the Settings page and the (empty-state) Net Worth dashboard in both themes confirmed real contrast, not just class presence, which is what caught the nav-header bug above. Script and downloaded artifacts were removed afterward; nothing left in the working tree.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1624 tests (14 new, in `theme.test.js`) run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Font family and text size preferences — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:font-family-and-text-size-preferences -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Text size is a multiplier on `<html>`'s `font-size`, not a per-element override.** `--text-scale` (`1` default, `0.875` small, `1.125` large) feeds `html { font-size: calc(var(--text-scale) * 100%) }`; since Tailwind's `text-*` utilities are `rem`-based, every element already using them scales app-wide from this one rule. This is exactly the shape the issue asked for ("a global scale... variable that Tailwind's typography utilities already key off," not per-component overrides), and it needed no changes to any existing component.
- **Font family is a `--app-font-family` custom property, reassigned per class, that only `body` reads.** `:root` sets it to a `--font-sans-stack` copied verbatim from Tailwind v4's own `--font-sans` default (`node_modules/tailwindcss/theme.css`), so the "no preference chosen" appearance is pixel-identical to before this issue; `.font-serif`/`.font-rounded` repoint it to `--font-serif-stack`/`--font-rounded-stack`. `body` (not `html`) is where `font-family` is actually declared: Tailwind v4's preflight sets `html`'s `font-family` via its own build-time `--theme()` resolution (`node_modules/tailwindcss/preflight.css`), which isn't guaranteed to stay a live, overridable `var()` reference after compilation — declaring it explicitly on `body`, which normally just inherits from `html` anyway, sidesteps needing to know or rely on that compiled output.
- **`src/lib/typography.js` is two independent preferences in one module, following `src/lib/theme.js`'s exact shape (issue #116) end to end**, per the issue's explicit "coordinate the persistence approach with it" instruction: a `hasLocalStorage()` try/catch guard, module-level stores that start at a fixed default and are only made real by a `refreshTypography()` call from `onMount`, a `set*()` per preference that applies the `<html>` class, publishes to its store and persists to `localStorage`, and a matching inline script duplicated in `app.html` (two new keys, `uk-wealth-tracker:font-family` / `uk-wealth-tracker:text-size`) so the first paint is never a flash of the wrong font/size before hydration.
- **No stored choice resolves to the app's existing default appearance, not a system preference.** Theme has `prefers-color-scheme` to fall back on; there is no OS-level signal for "preferred font family" or "preferred base text size" a browser exposes, so `resolveFontFamily()`/`resolveTextSize()` simply return `DEFAULT_FONT_FAMILY`/`DEFAULT_TEXT_SIZE` (`'sans'`/`'medium'`) when nothing is stored — matching what every visitor already saw before this issue existed.
- **`TypographySettings.svelte` is a new component, not an addition to `ThemeSettings.svelte`.** The issue's mechanism note ("coordinate... if that issue lands first") is about persistence approach, not UI grouping; `ThemeSettings`/`ThemeToggleButton` are already split by *where* they appear (Settings page vs. nav), and this preference has no nav-accessible quick-toggle equivalent to justify sharing a component. It sits on the Settings page directly below `ThemeSettings`, styled the same two-button-group way (`Card`, `Button` with `variant={selected ? 'default' : 'outline'}`).
- **Two button groups (Font, Text size) inside one `Card`, not two separate cards.** Both are "typography," and the issue frames them as one Settings section ("Add typography preferences... a font choice... and a text-size scale"); splitting them into separate cards would visually imply they're as unrelated as theme vs. typography rather than two facets of the same setting.
- **No live font preview inside the option buttons themselves.** An earlier draft added the target font's class to each button's own label (so "Serif" rendered in serif), but `--app-font-family` is only consumed by `body`'s `font-family` declaration — an element's own custom-property value doesn't retroactively change a `font-family` a parent already computed and it inherited, so a button carrying `.font-serif` locally would not actually render in serif without redundantly repeating `font-family: var(--app-font-family)` on every option button too. Dropped rather than added: the plain button labels `ThemeSettings.svelte` uses (no colour swatches previewing "Light"/"Dark" either) are the existing pattern.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Three font choices exactly as the issue suggested ("e.g. the current default sans, a serif option, and a rounded/more-legible option"), not a fourth "system" or "monospace" option.** The issue explicitly asks for "a small curated set... not an open-ended picker."
- **Text size step values (0.875 / 1 / 1.125, i.e. 14px / 16px / 18px at the browser default) are a judgement call** — the issue names the three tiers ("small/medium/large") but not a scale factor. These mirror the ~12.5% step Tailwind's own `text-sm`→`text-base`→`text-lg` progression uses, so "large" reads as a deliberate step up rather than a barely-perceptible nudge, while staying small enough that no layout in the app (checked via the browser drive below) visibly breaks.
- **`--font-rounded-stack` (`ui-rounded, 'SF Pro Rounded', Nunito, 'Segoe UI', Roboto, sans-serif`) has no universally-installed rounded system font to anchor on the way serif has Georgia/Times.** `ui-rounded` (Safari-only rounded system-font keyword) leads, `SF Pro Rounded` covers Apple platforms directly, `Nunito` is a widely-available rounded webfont-adjacent name some systems substitute, and the chain falls back to the same sans stack otherwise — most browsers on most OSes will render this as a rounded-looking sans face at best and the plain default sans at worst, which is an acceptable "best effort, no new font files" reading of "a rounded/more-legible option" given the issue's own "not an open-ended picker" framing rules out shipping a webfont.
- **No dedicated component test file for `TypographySettings.svelte`** — consistent with `ThemeSettings.svelte`, which also has none; both are thin, near-identical wrappers over a well-tested `lib` module (`typography.test.js` here, 19 new tests covering every exported function and both the "applies a class" and "removes the class going back to default" directions), and the browser drive below exercises the actual component rendering and click handling end to end.
- **Driven for real in a browser**, continuing the pattern from #22–#38/#98/#61/#62/#63/#100/#28/#31/#33/#39/#64/#111/#116. `npm run build` + `vite preview`, headless system Chromium over the DevTools Protocol (`playwright-core` installed with `npm install --no-save`, not added to `package.json`/the lockfile). Confirmed on the Settings page: clicking "Serif" flips `<html class="font-serif">` and `body`'s computed `font-family` to the serif stack; clicking "Large" additionally sets `text-scale-large` and changes `<html>`'s computed `font-size` from `16px` to `18px`; both choices persist to their `localStorage` keys and survive a full page reload (`app.html`'s inline script re-applying them before hydration, not just `onMount` catching up after a flash); reverting to both "Default" options removes both classes and restores `16px`/the sans stack exactly. A second run confirmed Rounded + Small + Dark mode together resolve to `<html class="font-rounded text-scale-small dark">` with no conflict between the two features' classes, and a full-page screenshot of the Net Worth dashboard with Serif + Large active showed the nav, headings and body copy all rendering in the larger serif face — not just the Settings page's own text. No console messages or page errors in any run; script and its Chromium profile directory were removed afterward, nothing left in the working tree.
- **The pre-existing `<meta name="text-scale" content="scale" />` line in `app.html` was removed.** It shipped in the original scaffold commit, is not a real HTML meta name browsers or any tooling recognise, and did nothing — plausibly a placeholder dropped while scaffolding this exact issue's name. Since this issue touches this file's `<head>` directly for the real mechanism, leaving a same-named no-op tag sitting next to the feature it resembles but isn't part of would read as broken or duplicated wiring to the next person to look at this file.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1643 tests (19 new, in `typography.test.js`) run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Emoji prefix added to both the nav tab labels (in `src/lib/nav.js`) and the matching page heading in each route's `+page.svelte`.** This ensures visual consistency across the entire app — the emoji appears in the navigation sidebar and on every page the user lands on.
- **Emoji choices follow the issue's suggested list exactly:** 💰 Net Worth, 📈 Forecast, 🏖️ Retirement, 🧾 Tax, 👴 Pensions, 💷 Dividends, 🏠 Property, 💎 Assets, 📅 Budget, 🏛️ Estate, ⚙️ Settings. All emoji are one character wide (with the 8️⃣ variant selector included for emoji like 🏖️) and easy to scan at a glance.

All 9 nav tests pass; all 1624 tests in the full suite pass.

The Settings emoji (⚙️) is consistent with common UI conventions and is clearly distinguishable from feature tabs.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **No changes made to test files.** The nav tests already pass because they match on `href`, not label text. No new test assertions were needed — the existing test suite is sufficient to catch any regressions.
- **No dedicated emoji-lookup module created.** A simpler approach — inline emoji strings in `nav.js` labels and copying them into each page's `<h1>` — was chosen over storing emoji separately and having both places reference a shared definition, to keep the implementation minimal and the code easy to navigate without hunting across files.

- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds

- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Added a GitHub icon link (🔗) in the header, positioned beside the GitHub connection indicator and theme toggle.** This provides users with quick access to the source code repository without leaving the application. The link uses the same button styling pattern as the `ThemeToggleButton` component for visual consistency.
- **The link opens `https://github.com/mmorrow24work/uk-wealth-tracker` in a new tab with `target="_blank"` and `rel="noopener noreferrer"`.** The `rel` attribute prevents the new tab from accessing the `window.opener` object, a security best practice for external links.
- **The button uses a chain-link emoji (🔗) with a screen-reader label ("GitHub repository") for accessibility.** This matches the existing pattern used by `ThemeToggleButton` (sun/moon emoji with labels) and ensures the control is usable and understandable for all users.
- **No new dependencies were added.** The solution uses only the existing HTML `<a>` element and does not require any additional libraries or components.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Used a chain-link emoji (🔗) instead of an octocat icon or GitHub's mark.** Emoji are simpler to implement than SVG icons and are consistent with the emoji-first design of the navigation (issue #118 added emoji to nav tabs). A proper GitHub icon from shadcn-svelte would require additional setup and component extraction, while emoji are immediately available, small, and universally recognizable.
- **The link is not in the nav menu itself but in the header's right-aligned area with the GitHub connection and theme controls.** This positions it as a utility function alongside other non-tab navigation items, which is the appropriate place for a "go to repo" action that users would expect to find alongside account/settings controls, not mixed with feature tabs.

- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **All seven palettes landed in one pass rather than taking the issue's suggested "infrastructure + 2-3 palettes first" split.** The expensive part of this issue turned out to be the *verification* harness, not the palettes: once `src/palettes.css.test.js` could parse `app.css` and recompute every contrast ratio, adding the sixth and seventh palette cost a few lines of hue each and the test told me immediately whether they cleared the bar. Splitting would have meant building that harness once and then spending a second session' worth of context re-establishing why the numbers are what they are.
- **A palette is a `.palette-<name>` class on `<html>` that swaps the same CSS custom properties `.dark` already swaps — so the two axes multiply rather than conflict.** Every palette ships two rules, `.palette-x` (light) and `.dark.palette-x` (dark); `$lib/theme.js` keeps toggling `.dark` and knows nothing about palettes, `$lib/palette.js` keeps toggling one `palette-*` class and knows nothing about light/dark. Switching palette therefore never kicks you out of dark mode, which is the specific failure the issue warned against.
- **Rule order in `app.css` is load-bearing and is asserted by a test, not just a comment.** `.palette-beach` and `.dark` are both specificity 0-1-0, so if the light palettes were emitted *after* `.dark`, a dark page would be repainted light by an equal-weight later rule; `.dark.palette-beach` is 0-2-0 and wins wherever it sits, but is kept after `.dark` so the file reads in cascade order. `palettes.css.test.js` compares the three rules' byte offsets, because "someone tidied the file by grouping all the beach rules together" is exactly the plausible future edit that would silently break dark mode for every named palette.
- **The palettes were designed numerically, not by eye.** Each is generated from a small recipe — a surface hue, a chrome hue, an accent hue, three saturations — so a palette is internally consistent by construction and only the hues differ between them; the individual lightness steps were then adjusted against a contrast checker until every pair cleared its bar. `--chart-1`/`--chart-2` are chosen per palette rather than inherited, since the default blue/orange pair clashes badly on a green or plum surface.
- **`src/palettes.css.test.js` recomputes contrast from the CSS itself rather than asserting known-good hex values.** It parses `app.css`, resolves each palette/variant the way a browser's cascade would (`:root` → `.palette-x` → `.dark` → `.dark.palette-x`), and checks ten foreground/background token pairs at 4.5:1, both chart series at 3:1 against both surfaces they're drawn on, and the two series at CIELAB ΔE ≥ 8 from each other under normal vision plus protanope, deuteranope and tritanope simulation. That last check is the one the issue implied but didn't spell out: two hues can each clear 3:1 against the card and still collapse into one colour for a red-green-blind reader, which is the failure mode a green-and-brown "country" palette walks straight into (its first draft did, at ΔE 7.4 in dark mode, and the test caught it).
- **Two pre-existing default-palette values were nudged to clear AA, so the rule is "every palette", with no exception.** `--muted-foreground` at 45.1% was 4.35:1 against `--muted` — which `bg-muted text-muted-foreground` pairings actually render (DebtTracker's "unknown" badge, AutoInvestFill's chips) — and white on the old `--destructive` red was 3.76:1, which the `destructive` button variant puts on screen in DeleteAllData's confirm button. 43.5% and a 45%-lightness red fix both. A test that had to carve out "…except the default one" would have been worth much less.
- **`.palette-default` exists in the CSS as a second selector on `:root`, purely so the Settings picker can draw a live "Default" swatch.** Each swatch is an element carrying its own palette class, so `.palette-beach`'s custom properties apply *to the swatch* and the inline `hsl(var(--primary))` styles inside resolve to beach's colours without the page changing — theming with custom properties gets that for free. The default option had no class to reset with and would have inherited whatever palette `<html>` was on; sharing `:root`'s selector beats restating nineteen values in a second rule. `<html>` itself never carries `.palette-default` — no class at all is the default, same as `.font-serif` and `.text-scale-small`.
- **The swatches take `class:dark` too when the page is dark.** `.dark.palette-x` needs both classes on one element, so without it every swatch would preview its light variant on a dark page — showing the user a colour scheme they wouldn't get if they clicked it.
- **`src/lib/palette.js` follows `theme.js`/`typography.js` end to end**: `localStorage` under its own key (`uk-wealth-tracker:palette`, deliberately outside the Gist-synced `AppData` document, since this is a per-browser display preference), a `hasLocalStorage()` try/catch guard, a store that starts at the default and is only made real by a `refreshPalette()` call from `onMount`, and a matching inline script in `app.html` so the first paint is never the wrong palette. Verified in a browser: after a reload, `<html>` carries `palette-beach` before hydration, not after.
- **The palette name list now exists in three places (the module, the CSS, `app.html`'s inline script), so a test pins them to each other.** The inline script can't import the module — that's why the duplication exists at all — so `palettes.css.test.js` asserts the script's array, `PALETTES`, and the set of `.palette-*` rules all name the same seven palettes, and that the script reads the same `localStorage` key and builds the same class names the module writes.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The `Male` and `Female` labels are kept as the issue wrote them, with the framing moved into the UI copy rather than the names.** Each is described by its actual colours ("Cool slate blues with a teal accent", "Warm plums and violets over blush"), they sit side by side with neither implied as a default, and the hues are deep and muted rather than pastel-pink/sports-blue. Renaming them "Cool"/"Warm" was tempting and would arguably have been better UI, but the issue names them explicitly and being able to find the palette you were told about matters more than my preference about the label.
- **Palettes deliberately do not restate `--destructive`/`--destructive-foreground`.** Red-means-danger is semantic rather than decorative; a beach-tinted "delete everything" button would be worse design, not better theming. Every palette inherits the one red.
- **The status colours scattered through the components as literal Tailwind classes (`text-red-600`, `text-amber-700`, `bg-green-50`, …) are untouched and stay the same in every palette.** Retinting ~40 call sites across a dozen components was well outside what "add a set of named colour-palette themes" asks for, and they are semantic in the same way `--destructive` is. It does mean an amber warning on the country palette's parchment surface is a slightly odd pairing; converting those to tokens is its own issue.
- **No nav quick-switcher for palettes.** Light/dark has one because it's a single binary flip you want mid-task; eight named options with swatches is a Settings-page control, and the issue asks for exactly that ("selectable from Settings").
- **Contrast is verified against the tokens, not against rendered pixels.** The test proves `--muted-foreground` clears 4.5:1 on `--muted`; it can't prove no component stacks a `/40` opacity modifier over an unexpected surface (several do, e.g. `bg-muted/40` panels). Those were checked by eye in the browser drive rather than by assertion — a real pixel-sampling accessibility audit is a bigger tool than this issue justifies.
- **`--border`/`--input` are not held to any contrast floor**, in any palette including the default, which has always drawn a 1.2:1 hairline border. Raising that is a visible redesign of every card and input in the app, not a palette change.
- **No dedicated component test for `PaletteSettings.svelte`**, consistent with `ThemeSettings.svelte` and `TypographySettings.svelte`, which also have none — all three are thin wrappers over a well-tested `lib` module (`palette.test.js` here, 19 new tests), and the browser drive below exercises the component's rendering and click handling end to end.
- **Driven for real in a browser**, continuing the pattern from #22–#38/#98/#61/#62/#63/#100/#28/#31/#33/#39/#64/#111/#116/#117. `npm run build` + `vite preview`, headless system Chromium over `playwright-core` (installed with `npm install --no-save`, not added to `package.json`/the lockfile). Confirmed on the Settings page: clicking each of the seven palettes swaps exactly one `palette-*` class on `<html>`, repaints `body`'s computed background/colour and the primary button fill, and persists to `localStorage`; reloading comes back on `palette-beach` with no flash, applied by the inline script before hydration; "Dark" on top of a palette yields `<html class="palette-beach dark">` with the dark variant's warm near-black surfaces and turquoise accents, and navigating to the Net Worth dashboard carries the palette across routes; Cricket + Dark + Serif together resolve to `<html class="dark font-serif palette-cricket">` with all three features composing and no conflict; "Default" removes the class and restores the original white/near-black exactly. Each swatch renders its own palette's colours in both modes. No console messages or page errors in any run; the script and `playwright-core` were removed afterward, nothing left in the working tree.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 1729 tests (86 new: 19 in `palette.test.js`, 67 in `palettes.css.test.js`) run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.
